People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I keep thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up… and sometimes when I’m driving home from the clinic, I catch myself wondering what would happen if I just didn’t turn the wheel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel while driving?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now. This whole intense up-and-down crisis around taking in our relative has been going on that long, and the thoughts have been part of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the time since these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days it feels like all the color has gone out of everything. Even when the house is busy, I just feel… hopeless. I’ll go hide in the laundry room and cry because I don’t want anyone to see me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I haven’t really touched my camera, and I used to love taking pictures. I’ll see the bird feeders and not even care to check them, and I haven’t been interested in what’s coming up in the garden either. Those were the things that made me feel like myself, so it’s honestly frightening.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of heightened activity or irritability over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Earlier this month there was a stretch where I felt almost… electrically upbeat, like I was running on a charge. But I was also really irritable. I snapped at people over tiny things, like a medication cup being in the wrong place or someone changing the morning routine. It didn’t feel like how I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual or having trouble getting started on things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel like everything takes so much effort. At work, just walking from the treatment area to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. And when I get home, I can barely get dinner made or help with the evening care routine. It’s like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how much weight I’ve lost, if any. My appetite has been really up and down, though. Lately I mostly just pick at toast or have coffee, and then I’ll get to the end of a clinic shift and realize I barely ate all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m scared of where my thoughts have been going. I keep thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up… and sometimes when I’m driving home from the clinic, I catch myself wondering what would happen if I just didn’t turn the wheel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel while driving?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now. This whole intense up-and-down crisis around taking in our relative has been going on that long, and the thoughts have been part of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the time since these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days it feels like all the color has gone out of everything. Even when the house is busy, I just feel… hopeless. I’ll go hide in the laundry room and cry because I don’t want anyone to see me like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I haven’t really touched my camera, and I used to love taking pictures. I’ll see the bird feeders and not even care to check them, and I haven’t been interested in what’s coming up in the garden either. Those were the things that made me feel like myself, so it’s honestly frightening.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of heightened activity or irritability over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Earlier this month there was a stretch where I felt almost… electrically upbeat, like I was running on a charge. But I was also really irritable. I snapped at people over tiny things, like a medication cup being in the wrong place or someone changing the morning routine. It didn’t feel like how I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual or having trouble getting started on things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel like everything takes so much effort. At work, just walking from the treatment area to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. And when I get home, I can barely get dinner made or help with the evening care routine. It’s like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how much weight I’ve lost, if any. My appetite has been really up and down, though. Lately I mostly just pick at toast or have coffee, and then I’ll get to the end of a clinic shift and realize I barely ate all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual perceptions, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, over the past month?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t heard voices or seen things that other people couldn’t. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I used to look forward to taking photos, checking the bird feeders, seeing what was coming up in the garden… those were kind of my things. Lately I haven’t touched my camera, and I don’t really care about any of it. That scares me, because those are the things that used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure, even if it's not as much as before?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no… not really. Maybe for a second here or there, but I haven’t touched my camera, and I’ll look at the bird feeders without really caring. Same with the garden—I used to be excited to see what was coming up, and now it just feels like nothing. That’s been frightening, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find pleasurable at all right now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the things I used to enjoy, I’m just not doing them. I haven’t touched my camera, I’ll look at the bird feeders but I don’t really care, and I’m not interested in what’s coming up in the garden. It’s frightening, honestly, because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel like I just can’t get pleasure from the things that normally made me happy. I haven’t touched my camera, I don’t really care about the bird feeders, and I’m not interested in what’s coming up in the garden. Earlier this month there was a stretch where I felt almost… electrically upbeat, like I was running on a charge. But I was also really irritable. I snapped at people over tiny things, like a medication cup being in the wrong place or someone changing the morning routine. It didn’t feel like how I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual or having trouble getting started on things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel like everything takes so much effort. At work, just walking from the treatment area to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. And when I get home, I can barely get dinner made or help with the evening care routine. It’s like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how much weight I’ve lost, if any. My appetite has been really up and down, though. Lately I mostly just pick at toast or have coffee, and then I’ll get to the end of a clinic shift and realize I barely ate all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual perceptions, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, over the past month?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t heard voices or seen things that other people couldn’t. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I used to look forward to taking photos, checking the bird feeders, seeing what was coming up in the garden… those were kind of my things. Lately I haven’t touched my camera, and I don’t really care about any of it. That scares me, because those are the things that used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure, even if it's not as much as before?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no… not really. Maybe for a second here or there, but I haven’t touched my camera, and I’ll look at the bird feeders without really caring. Same with the garden—I used to be excited to see what was coming up, and now it just feels like nothing. That’s been frightening, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find pleasurable at all right now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the things I used to enjoy, I’m just not doing them. I haven’t touched my camera, I’ll look at the bird feeders but I don’t really care, and I’m not interested in what’s coming up in the garden. It’s frightening, honestly, because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel like I just can’t get pleasure from the things that normally made me happy. I haven’t touched my camera, I don’t really care about the bird feeders, and I’m not interested in what’s coming up in the garden. It’s frightening, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts of not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel are associated with a wish to die?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. I don’t have some detailed plan, but the thought is there that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And when I’m driving home and I wonder what would happen if I didn’t turn the wheel, that scares me. It feels like part of me doesn’t want to keep doing this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past month where you felt completely fine, with no depressive symptoms at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. It’s been hanging over me for about 11 months now… and this past month has just made it feel unbearable. But I can’t say I’ve had a stretch where I felt completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past month where your depressive symptoms significantly worsened?\n\nPatient: Yes. The last month has been much worse. This whole crisis around taking in our relative has had me going up and down intensely, and it’s been going on for about a month now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I feel like everything takes so much effort. At work, just walking from the treatment area to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. And when I get home, I can barely get dinner made or help with the evening care routine. It’s like I can’t get myself going.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how much weight I’ve lost, if any. My appetite has been really up and down, though. Lately I mostly just pick at toast or have coffee, and then I’ll get to the end of a clinic shift and realize I barely ate all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual perceptions, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, over the past month?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t heard voices or seen things that other people couldn’t. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I used to look forward to taking photos, checking the bird feeders, seeing what was coming up in the garden… those were kind of my things. Lately I haven’t touched my camera, and I don’t really care about any of it. That scares me, because those are the things that used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a bit of joy or pleasure, even if it's not as much as before?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no… not really. Maybe for a second here or there, but I haven’t touched my camera, and I’ll look at the bird feeders without really caring. Same with the garden—I used to be excited to see what was coming up, and now it just feels like nothing. That’s been frightening, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find pleasurable at all right now?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the things I used to enjoy, I’m just not doing them. I haven’t touched my camera, I’ll look at the bird feeders but I don’t really care, and I’m not interested in what’s coming up in the garden. It’s frightening, honestly, because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel like I just can’t get pleasure from the things that normally made me happy. I haven’t touched my camera, I don’t really care about the bird feeders, and I’m not interested in what’s coming up in the garden. It’s frightening, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts of not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel are associated with a wish to die?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. I don’t have some detailed plan, but the thought is there that it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And when I’m driving home and I wonder what would happen if I didn’t turn the wheel, that scares me. It feels like part of me doesn’t want to keep doing this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past month where you felt completely fine, with no depressive symptoms at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. It’s been hanging over me for about 11 months now… and this past month has just made it feel unbearable. But I can’t say I’ve had a stretch where I felt completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past month where your depressive symptoms significantly worsened?\n\nPatient: Yes. The last month has been much worse. This whole crisis around taking in our relative has had me going up and down intensely, and it’s been going on for about a month now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your depressive symptoms have been present for most of the day, every day, over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes… for the most part. This intense up-and-down around taking in our relative has been going on for about a month, and it feels like it’s been there pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your depressive symptoms over the past month as mild, moderate, or severe?\n\nPatient: Severe. It’s affecting everything. I’ve started making mistakes at the clinic, and I’ve been avoiding friends because I’m afraid I’ll either snap at someone or just fall apart. At home, my husband’s had to take over the bills, meals… and even kind of keep an eye on me when my thoughts get really dark.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’ll see the bird feeders and not even care to check them, and I haven’t been interested in what’s coming up in the garden either.
